<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### The theorem and the structure of the proof

How quickly does local resampling remove fluctuations in a densely coupled spin system? For the Sherrington–Kirkpatrick model <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[SK1975](#ref-SK1975)], the signs of the interactions matter: each spin interacts weakly with every other spin, but the sum of the absolute interaction strengths grows with the dimension. We study heat-bath dynamics, which updates a spin from its conditional Gibbs law, in the tradition of Glauber’s local stochastic dynamics <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[Glauber1963](#ref-Glauber1963)]. Our result is a dimension-independent Poincaré inequality throughout the high-temperature phase, at each fixed inverse temperature below one. It controls every real observable on a single event of disorder probability tending to one.

<a id="eq:dirichlet"></a>
Fix $0<\beta<1$ and put $j=\beta^2$. Let $J$ be a real symmetric $n\times n$ matrix with zero diagonal and independent off-diagonal entries $J_{ij}\sim N(0,j/n)$, $i<j$. For a field $h\in\mathbb R^n$, write $$\mu_h(x)=\frac{1}{Z_h}\exp\left\{\frac12x^{\mathsf T}Jx+h^{\mathsf T}x\right\},
 \qquad x\in\{-1,1\}^n.$$ Let $P_i^h$ denote conditional expectation under $\mu_h$ given all spins except the $i$th. Define the unscaled Dirichlet form $$\label{eq:dirichlet}
 \mathcal D_h(f)=\sum_{i=1}^n\mathbb E_{\mu_h}(f-P_i^h f)^2.$$

We use a rate-one clock at each site in continuous time, giving the generator $\mathcal L_h=\sum_i(P_i^h-I)$. A discrete attempt selects one site uniformly and has transition operator $P_h=n^{-1}\sum_iP_i^h$. Thus $\mathcal L_h=n(P_h-I)$, and the discrete gap is the continuous gap divided by $n$. The gap of a reversible generator is the infimum of its Dirichlet form divided by the variance, over all nonconstant functions.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *There is a finite constant $C_\beta$, depending only on $\beta$, such that $$\mathbb P_J\left\{\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}_{\mu_0}(f)\le C_\beta\mathcal D_0(f)
       \text{ for every }f:\{-1,1\}^n\longrightarrow\mathbb R\right\}
 \longrightarrow1.$$ Consequently, the discrete-time heat-bath chain that chooses a uniform site has spectral gap at least $1/(C_\beta n)$ with probability tending to one.*

</div>

The parameter $\beta$ is fixed before $n$ tends to infinity; the constant $C_\beta$ may depend on that fixed choice. External fields enter the proof through posterior Gibbs laws, while the theorem concerns $\mu_0$. The diagonal convention is immaterial to the Gibbs law: because $x_i^2=1$, adding any diagonal matrix to $J$ adds a constant to the Hamiltonian. This observation will let us introduce a Gaussian diagonal for matrix calculations and remove it again.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### Earlier results and methods

The interval $\beta<1$ is the classical zero-field high-temperature range. Aizenman, Lebowitz, and Ruelle <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[ALR1987](#ref-ALR1987), Proposition 2.1] proved that $n^{-1}\log Z_0$ converges in probability and in mean to $\log2+\beta^2/4$, the annealed free-energy value, throughout this interval. The dynamical question is whether local resampling also removes fluctuations at a rate independent of the system size.

A basic route to such a bound controls conditional influences. Wu <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[Wu2006](#ref-Wu2006)] proved a Poincaré inequality under a Dobrushin spectral-radius condition on a matrix of nonnegative conditional influences. In dense SK systems, stronger fixed-temperature estimates become possible by exploiting the signed random interactions. Bauerschmidt and Bodineau <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[BB2019](#ref-BB2019)] used a Gaussian field decomposition to prove a high-temperature logarithmic Sobolev inequality; its full spin-flip energy differs from the heat-bath form <a href="#eq:dirichlet" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dirichlet">[eq:dirichlet]</a>. Eldan, Koehler, and Zeitouni <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[EKZ2022](#ref-EKZ2022)] established a spectral condition for the heat-bath gap, yielding the SK range $\beta<1/4$. Adhikari, Brennecke, Xu, and Yau <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[ABXY2024](#ref-ABXY2024)] obtained constant unscaled gaps for mixed $p$-spin models under a sufficiently small weighted condition on their interaction coefficients.

For mixing in single-site discrete attempts, Anari, Jain, Koehler, Pham, and Vuong <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[AJKPV2022](#ref-AJKPV2022)] used entropic independence to prove an $O(n\log n)$ bound in the SK range $\beta<1/4$. Anari, Koehler, and Vuong <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[AKV2024](#ref-AKV2024)] extended this order to an inverse-temperature threshold approximately equal to $0.295$. More recently, Wang <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[Wang2026](#ref-Wang2026), Theorem 1.1] proved worst-start mixing in $O_\beta(n\log(n/\varepsilon))$ attempts for every fixed $\beta<1/2$, simultaneously over external fields. Boban, Li, and Oveis Gharan <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[BLO2026](#ref-BLO2026), Theorem 1.5 and Corollary 1.6] obtained a constant unscaled gap and $O(n^2)$ zero-field mixing for $\beta<1/2+\varepsilon_0$, with an absolute $\varepsilon_0\ge5\cdot10^{-5}$. Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> reaches every fixed $\beta<1$ at zero field.

Equilibrium covariance estimates already cover this entire range. El Alaoui and Gaitonde <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[EAG2024](#ref-EAG2024), Theorem 1.1] proved that the expected operator norm of the zero-field spin covariance matrix stays bounded for every fixed $\beta<1$. Brennecke, Schertzer, Xu, and Yau <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[BSXY2024](#ref-BSXY2024), Theorem 1.1] proved the operator-norm approximation $$\left\|\mathop{\mathrm{Cov}}_{\mu_0}(x)-\bigl((1+\beta^2)I-J\bigr)^{-1}\right\|
 \longrightarrow0
 \qquad\text{in probability}.$$ For $a\in\mathbb R^n$, the variance of the linear observable $a^{\mathsf T}x$ is $a^{\mathsf T}\mathop{\mathrm{Cov}}_{\mu_0}(x)a$. These covariance results therefore control linear fluctuations. The all-functions heat-bath inequality requires additional estimates; our proof obtains them at the random posterior fields generated by Gaussian observations.

Our argument transfers a functional inequality along stochastic localization, introduced by Eldan <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[Eldan2013](#ref-Eldan2013)]. We use its Gaussian-observation representation developed by El Alaoui and Montanari <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[ElAlaouiMontanari2021](#ref-ElAlaouiMontanari2021)]. The variance-retention and Dirichlet-form framework of Chen and Eldan <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[CE2025](#ref-CE2025)] explains how posterior estimates can imply an inequality for the initial law. Here an observation event of exponentially small probability can still carry substantial variance for a particular test function. We control its probability after weighting paths by the square of that function. The entropy calculation is a stopped finite-mixture version of the drift-energy representation; see Lehec <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[Lehec2013](#ref-Lehec2013)] and the work of Föllmer discussed there.

Gaussian observations, planted laws, and approximate message passing (AMP) are also combined in the SK sampling method of El Alaoui, Montanari, and Sellke <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[EAMS2022](#ref-EAMS2022)]. Celentano <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[Celentano2024](#ref-Celentano2024)] proved local convexity of the TAP free energy used by that sampler near sufficiently late AMP iterates. Our argument needs invertibility at every sufficiently accurate solution of the field equation along the observation path. A scalar entropy inequality and a local-volume estimate give this uniformity in Section <a href="#sec:stability" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:stability">5</a>. The field recursions retain the Onsager correction of the Thouless–Anderson–Palmer equations <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[TAP1977](#ref-TAP1977)] and are related to Bolthausen’s iterative construction <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[Bolthausen2014](#ref-Bolthausen2014)]. We initialize them at a Gibbs spin sample and use exact conditional-spin identities through a fixed finite depth, without requiring convergence of the recursion. Finally, the terminal gap adapts Wang’s signed two-spin method <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[Wang2026](#ref-Wang2026)]; Section <a href="#sec:terminal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:terminal">8</a> proves the variant with an entrywise-square cost and a quartic row error.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### Notation and elementary identities

For a vector $v$, set $\langle v\rangle=n^{-1}\sum_i v_i$ and let $D_v$ be the diagonal matrix with diagonal $v$. Vector products and scalar functions of vectors are understood coordinatewise. Unless another subscript is displayed, $\lVert \cdot\rVert$ is Euclidean norm for vectors and operator norm for matrices; $\lVert \cdot\rVert_{\mathrm F}$ is Frobenius norm. A centered Gaussian orthogonal ensemble (GOE) matrix of scale $j$ has variances $j/n$ off diagonal and $2j/n$ on the diagonal.

<a id="eq:discrete-product"></a>
If $x^{(i)}$ is obtained by flipping the $i$th spin, put $$d_i f(x)=\frac{f(x)-f(x^{(i)})}{2x_i},\qquad
 (\nabla u)_{ij}=d_j u_i.$$ Thus $d_i f$ is independent of $x_i$ and $$\label{eq:discrete-product}
 d_i(fg)=f\,d_i g+g\,d_i f-2x_i(d_i f)(d_i g).$$ At a fixed field $h_0$ set $$h^1=h_0+Jx,\qquad m^1=\tanh h^1,\qquad v^1=\mathop{\mathrm{sech}}^2 h^1.$$ Since $J_{ii}=0$, $m_i^1$ and $v_i^1$ are respectively the conditional mean and variance of $x_i$. In particular, <a id="eq:dirichlet-gradient"></a>$$\label{eq:dirichlet-gradient}
 \mathcal D_{h_0}(f)=\mathbb E_{\mu_{h_0}}\sum_i v_i^1(d_i f)^2,\qquad
 \lVert G\rVert_*^2=\mathbb E_{\mu_{h_0}}G^2+\mathcal D_{h_0}(G).$$ For probability laws $\nu,\mu$, their relative entropy is $D(\nu\Vert\mu)=\int\log(\mathrm d\nu/\mathrm d\mu)\,\mathrm d\nu$ when $\nu\ll\mu$, and $+\infty$ otherwise.

Constants are independent of $n$; dependence on a fixed temperature, construction depth, or positive stability margin is recorded at its use. Their values may change between displays. The prerequisites are Gaussian concentration and comparison, Gaussian integration by parts, and stochastic calculus. We prove the model-specific estimates in the sections below.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
#### The proof mechanism

There are two parts to the argument: prove useful inequalities for posterior Gibbs laws, then transfer them to the unobserved law. A long but fixed observation makes most individual spins predictable. The corresponding small conditional variances, combined with a two-spin curvature inequality, give a terminal gap. To recover the initial variance, we need a directional covariance estimate along the path and a mean estimate under square reweighting. The former bounds the loss of variance; the latter prevents that variance from accumulating on exceptional paths.

The posterior estimates come from finite Onsager-corrected recursions. Exact conditional-spin identities control their residuals. A telescoping identity for the squared increments then selects two consecutive small increments without requiring convergence of the recursion. Uniform matrix estimates handle the resulting adaptive diagonal coefficients under an explicit inverse margin. To compare these approximate fields with an exact root, stability must hold throughout the small-residual region and persist when the diagonal variance matrix is replaced by the nearby response coefficients used in the comparison. A scalar entropy estimate controls a Gaussian integral, and a local-volume lower bound turns that integral control into exclusion of unstable approximate roots. All tolerances and finite depths are chosen before the large-$n$ limit.

<a id="pm-h-6"></a>
#### The three inputs

We observe a spin sample in independent Gaussian noise: <a id="eq:observation-process"></a>$$\label{eq:observation-process}
 X\sim\mu_0,\qquad Y(t)=tX+B(t),\quad 0\le t\le T,$$ where $B$ is standard $n$-dimensional Brownian motion independent of $X$. Conditional on the observations up to $t$, the spin law is $\mu_{Y(t)}$. The proof uses the following three inputs, established in the later sections.

1.  **Stability along ordinary observation paths.** Proposition <a href="#prop:stable-fields" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:stable-fields">9</a> gives a deterministic notion of a good field such that, for typical $J$, all fields $Y(t)$ are good except on an event of observation probability at most $e^{-a n}$, where $a>0$ is fixed.

<a id="eq:input-weighted"></a>
2.  **Two estimates at a good field.** For every $G$, Proposition <a href="#prop:directional" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:directional">23</a> proves <a id="eq:input-directional"></a>$$\label{eq:input-directional}
     \lVert \mathop{\mathrm{Cov}}_{\mu_h}(G,x)\rVert^2
     \le C\bigl(\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}_{\mu_h}(G)+\mathcal D_h(G)\bigr).$$ For $N=\mathbb E_{\mu_h}f^2>0$ and $\nu=f^2\mu_h/N$, Proposition <a href="#prop:weighted-mean" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:weighted-mean">24</a> proves, for each sufficiently small fixed $\delta>0$, $$\label{eq:input-weighted}
     \frac{\lVert \mathbb E_\nu x-t_*(h)\rVert}{\sqrt n}
     \le C\delta+\frac{C_\delta}{\sqrt n}
                     \left(1+\frac{\mathcal D_h(f)}{N}\right).$$ Here $t_*(h)=\tanh r(h)$, where $r(h)$ is the unique solution of $$r-h+\{j(1-n^{-1}\|\tanh r\|^2)I-J\}\tanh r=0.$$ Existence and uniqueness at the fields under consideration are part of Lemma <a href="#lem:root" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:root">15</a>. The constant $C$ is independent of $\delta$; $C_\delta$ may depend on it. Taking $f=1$ gives the same comparison for $\mathbb E_{\mu_h}x$.

3.  **A terminal gap.** For a sufficiently large fixed $T$, Proposition <a href="#prop:terminal-gap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:terminal-gap">27</a> gives $\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}_{\mu_{Y(T)}}f\le2\mathcal D_{Y(T)}(f)$ outside an event of ordinary observation probability at most $e^{-a n}$.

The transfer uses the two estimates for different purposes. Directional covariance controls posterior variance loss, while square-reweighted means bound the entropy of the changed observation law. Their combination controls the contribution of the bad paths before the terminal gap is applied. Section <a href="#sec:observation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:observation">2</a> proves this implication first, so the later technical sections have a precise target.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
#### Organization

After the observation transfer in Section <a href="#sec:observation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:observation">2</a>, Section <a href="#sec:matrices" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:matrices">3</a> constructs the matrix diagnostics used by the adaptive field calculations. Sections <a href="#sec:entropy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:entropy">4</a> and <a href="#sec:stability" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:stability">5</a> identify the scalar equality law and establish stability along observations. Section <a href="#sec:residuals" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:residuals">6</a> develops the finite recursions and their weak residual rules; Section <a href="#sec:consequences" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:consequences">7</a> uses them to prove the two posterior estimates. Section <a href="#sec:terminal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:terminal">8</a> closes the argument with the fixed-time terminal gap.

<a id="sec:observation"></a>
### From posterior estimates to a spectral gap

We now prove the transfer announced in the introduction. Its inputs are a covariance bound, a square-reweighted mean bound, and a terminal gap. The stopping rule and good-disorder event must be independent of the test function, so we keep the exceptional-path estimate explicit. The argument uses the Gaussian-channel formulation of localization <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[ElAlaouiMontanari2021](#ref-ElAlaouiMontanari2021)] and the variance/Dirichlet-form framework of <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[CE2025](#ref-CE2025)]; the finite-spin identities are derived here.

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### Posterior martingales and Dirichlet forms

<a id="eq:filtering"></a>
Let $\mathcal F_t=\sigma(Y(s):s\le t)$ and write $\mathbb E_t$ for expectation under $\mu_{Y(t)}$. Bayes’ formula gives this posterior: the likelihood of an observation path given $X=x$ is proportional to $\exp(x^{\mathsf T}Y(t)-tn/2)$. The process $$I(t)=Y(t)-\int_0^t\mathbb E_s x\,\mathrm ds$$ is the innovation Brownian motion in $\mathcal F_t$: conditional expectation makes it a continuous martingale, and its quadratic covariation is $tI_n$. The Brownian martingale characterization therefore applies. Applying Itô’s formula to the finitely many posterior weights gives, for every spin function $g$, $$\label{eq:filtering}
 \mathrm d(\mathbb E_t g)=\mathop{\mathrm{Cov}}_t(g,x)^{\mathsf T}\mathrm dI(t).$$ To verify the cancellation, differentiate the posterior expectation with respect to $Y$; its gradient is $\mathop{\mathrm{Cov}}_t(g,x)$. Each likelihood weight $\exp(x^{\mathsf T}Y(t)-tn/2)$ satisfies a stochastic exponential equation. The quotient rule for their weighted sums then gives <a href="#eq:filtering" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:filtering">[eq:filtering]</a>: subtracting $\mathbb E_t x\,\mathrm dt$ from $\mathrm dY$ removes the finite-variation term.

<a id="eq:dirichlet-monotonicity"></a>
At every deterministic time, $$\label{eq:dirichlet-monotonicity}
 \mathbb E\mathcal D_{Y(t)}(f)\le\mathcal D_0(f).$$ This is conditional-variance contraction: the $i$th summand on the left is $\mathbb E\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(f(X)\mid X_{-i},\mathcal F_t)$, and the one on the right is $\mathbb E\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(f(X)\mid X_{-i})$. The conditional variance decomposition gives the inequality after averaging over observations. In this section, an expectation without a field subscript refers to that experiment, with $J$ held fixed.

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### Stopping before stability fails

Stop before the first possible loss of the field estimates. More precisely, fix the terminal horizon $T$, take the observation stopping time $\tau_0$ from Corollary <a href="#cor:stopping" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:stopping">10</a>, and set $\tau=T\wedge\tau_0$. This rule does not use the test function. Before $\tau$, stability holds through residual threshold $\rho_0\sqrt n$, so both posterior estimates apply. On the specified matrix event, $\mathbb P(\tau_0\le T)\le e^{-an}$.

<a id="eq:ordinary-bad"></a>
For a fixed $J$, choose the canonical terminal good set $$\mathcal T_n(J)=\{h:\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}_{\mu_h}(g)\le2\mathcal D_h(g)\text{ for every }g\}.$$ Membership means positivity of a finite-dimensional symmetric quadratic form with coefficients continuous in $h$, so this set is Borel. Proposition <a href="#prop:terminal-gap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:terminal-gap">27</a> controls the probability that $Y(T)$ misses it. Declare the stopped path bad if $\tau_0\le T$, or if $\tau_0>T$ and $Y(T)\notin\mathcal T_n(J)$. The event is $\mathcal F_\tau$-measurable: before $T$ only stopping can make it bad, and at $T$ the remaining condition is determined by $Y(T)$. The two failure probabilities give, on the typical matrix events, $$\label{eq:ordinary-bad}
 \mathbb P(\mathrm{bad})\le 2e^{-a n}$$ for a fixed $a>0$, after decreasing the rate if necessary.

<a id="eq:variance-lower"></a>
Suppose $\mathbb E_{\mu_0}f=0$ and $\mathbb E_{\mu_0}f^2=1$. Set $$M_t=\mathbb E_t f,\quad N_t=\mathbb E_t f^2,\quad V_t=N_t-M_t^2,
 \quad d_0=\mathcal D_0(f).$$ By <a href="#eq:filtering" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:filtering">[eq:filtering]</a> and optional stopping, $u(t)=\mathbb EV_{t\wedge\tau}$ is absolutely continuous and satisfies, almost everywhere, $$u'(t)=-\mathbb E\bigl[\mathbf 1_{\{t<\tau\}}\lVert \mathop{\mathrm{Cov}}_t(f,x)\rVert^2\bigr].$$ The directional estimate bounds the loss term before stopping. Together with <a href="#eq:dirichlet-monotonicity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dirichlet-monotonicity">[eq:dirichlet-monotonicity]</a>, applied at the deterministic time $t$, it gives $u'(t)\ge-C_{\mathrm d}(u(t)+d_0)$, with fixed $C_{\mathrm d}$. Solving this scalar inequality from $u(0)=1$ yields $$\label{eq:variance-lower}
 \mathbb EV_\tau\ge v-(1-v)d_0,\qquad v=e^{-C_{\mathrm d}T}>0.$$ All uses of optional stopping here are at bounded times and for bounded random variables at each fixed $n$.

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### Changing the law of the observations

<a id="eq:stopped-entropy"></a>
The variance lower bound alone does not control how much variance lies on bad paths. For that purpose change the initial spin law to $f^2\mu_0$, which is a probability law by normalization, and call the resulting observation law $\mathbb P^f$. Its density on $\mathcal F_t$ is $N_t$, and its spin posterior is $\nu_t=f^2\mu_{Y(t)}/N_t$. Since $\mu_{Y(t)}$ has full support and $f$ is nonzero, $N_t>0$. Bounded stopping gives the density $N_\tau$ on $\mathcal F_\tau$. Let $$a_t=\mathbb E_{\nu_t}x-\mathbb E_t x.$$ Equation <a href="#eq:filtering" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:filtering">[eq:filtering]</a> with $g=f^2$ gives $\mathrm dN_t/N_t=a_t^{\mathsf T}\mathrm dI(t)$. Under $\mathbb P^f$, $I$ has drift $a_t$. The drift-energy entropy identity, in the form discussed by Lehec <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[Lehec2013](#ref-Lehec2013)], has a direct finite-mixture proof here. Itô’s formula for $\log N_t$ yields $$\label{eq:stopped-entropy}
 D\bigl(\mathbb P^f|_{\mathcal F_\tau}\,\Vert\,
        \mathbb P|_{\mathcal F_\tau}\bigr)
 =\mathbb E^f\log N_\tau
 =\frac12\mathbb E^f\int_0^\tau\lVert a_t\rVert^2\,\mathrm dt.$$ The stopped identity needs no limiting integrability argument. At fixed $n$, the drift satisfies $\lVert a_t\rVert\le2\sqrt n$ and $\tau\le T$. Novikov’s condition therefore holds, and the stochastic integral in the log-density formula has zero mean.

<a id="eq:entropy-bound"></a>
<a id="eq:weighted-bad-bound"></a>
Before stopping, applying <a href="#eq:input-weighted" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:input-weighted">[eq:input-weighted]</a> to $f$ and to $1$ gives $$b_t:=\frac{\lVert a_t\rVert}{\sqrt n}
 \le2C_{\mathrm w}\delta+\frac{C_\delta}{\sqrt n}
                   \left(2+\frac{\mathcal D_{Y(t)}(f)}{N_t}\right).$$ Since $b_t\le2$, we may use $b_t^2\le2b_t$. At deterministic $t$, the density identity and <a href="#eq:dirichlet-monotonicity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dirichlet-monotonicity">[eq:dirichlet-monotonicity]</a> imply <a id="eq:weighted-dirichlet"></a>$$\label{eq:weighted-dirichlet}
 \mathbb E^f\frac{\mathcal D_{Y(t)}(f)}{N_t}
 =\mathbb E\mathcal D_{Y(t)}(f)\le d_0.$$ Write $\mathcal H_\tau$ for the entropy in <a href="#eq:stopped-entropy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:stopped-entropy">[eq:stopped-entropy]</a>. The preceding estimates give the quantitative bound $$\label{eq:entropy-bound}
 \frac{\mathcal H_\tau}{n}
 \le 2C_{\mathrm w}T\delta
       +\frac{C_\delta T}{\sqrt n}(2+d_0).$$ The separation of constants is essential: $C_{\mathrm w}$ is independent of $\delta$, whereas $C_\delta$ may grow when $\delta$ decreases. We next convert this entropy bound into a bound on the bad-path probability. If an event has probabilities $p$ and $q$ under two laws, the log-sum inequality for the event and its complement bounds relative entropy below by $p\log(1/q)-\log2$. With $p=\mathbb P^f(\mathrm{bad})$, <a href="#eq:ordinary-bad" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:ordinary-bad">[eq:ordinary-bad]</a> therefore gives, for large $n$, $$p\le\frac{\mathcal H_\tau+\log2}{an-\log2}.$$ If the ordinary bad probability is zero, absolute continuity gives $p=0$. For $d_0\le1$, <a href="#eq:entropy-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:entropy-bound">[eq:entropy-bound]</a> therefore implies $$\label{eq:weighted-bad-bound}
 p\le\frac{4C_{\mathrm w}T}{a}\delta
       +\frac{6C_\delta T}{a\sqrt n}
       +\frac{2\log2}{an}.$$ Choose $\delta>0$ once so that the first term is at most $v/8$. With this choice fixed, enlarge $n$ until the last two terms sum to at most $v/8$. The resulting bound holds simultaneously for all normalized $f$ with $d_0\le1$: <a id="eq:weighted-bad"></a>$$\label{eq:weighted-bad}
 \mathbb P^f(\mathrm{bad})\le v/4.$$

Figure <a href="#fig:observation-flow" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:observation-flow">1</a> records how the two posterior estimates will control the stopped variance and its exceptional contribution.

<figure id="fig:observation-flow">
<div class="tabular">
<p><span>c@c</span> &amp;<br />
<br />
<br />
&amp;<br />
&amp;<br />
</p>
</div>
<figcaption>Variance retention and control of exceptional paths, for a fixed typical matrix. Normalize $f$ by $𝔼_{μ0}f = 0$ and $𝔼_{μ0}f^{2} = 1$, and put $d_{0} = 𝒟_{0}(f) ≤ 1$. Along $Y(t) = tX + B(t)$, let $V_{t} = Var_{μY(t)}f$, $N_{t} = 𝔼_{μY(t)}f^{2}$, and $τ = T ∧ τ_{0}$. Here $ℋ_{τ}$ is the relative entropy of the stopped observation law induced by the spin law $f^{2}μ_{0}$, relative to the ordinary law, and $v = e^{−C_{d}T}$. The density $N_{τ}$ converts its bad-path probability into an upper bound on exceptional variance. If $d_{0} > 1$, the final lower bound is automatic.</figcaption>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-observation-flow"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`fig:observation-flow`)

Visual witness description of PDF page 9 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):

```text
A flow diagram showing variance retention and control of exceptional paths:
- Top level consists of two rectangular boxes side by side:
  - Left box labeled "Ordinary rarity" with formula $\mathbb{P}(\text{bad}) \le 2e^{-an}$.
  - Right box labeled "Weighted-mean estimate" with formula $\frac{H_\tau}{n} \le 2C_w T\delta + \frac{C_\delta T}{\sqrt{n}}(2 + d_0)$.
- A downward double arrow ($\Downarrow$) labeled "entropy inequality; choose $\delta$, then $n$" points from the top boxes to the middle box.
- Middle level consists of a wide rectangular box labeled "Variance on bad paths" containing $\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{1}_{\text{bad}} V_\tau] \le \mathbb{E}[\mathbf{1}_{\text{bad}} N_\tau] = \mathbb{P}^f(\text{bad}) \le v/4$.
- Below the middle box is a downward double arrow ($\Downarrow$) labeled "terminal gap on good paths" and formula $\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{1}_{\text{good}} V_\tau] \le 2d_0$.
- Bottom level consists of two rectangular boxes side by side:
  - Left box labeled "Directional covariance estimate" with formula $\mathbb{E} V_\tau \ge v - (1 - v)d_0$.
  - Right box labeled "Total remaining variance" with formula $\mathbb{E} V_\tau \le 2d_0 + v/4$.
- Below the two bottom boxes is the resulting inequality and deduction: $v - (1 - v)d_0 \le 2d_0 + v/4 \implies d_0 \ge v/4$.
```

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
#### Completion of the theorem

We can now close the argument, keeping the order of choices explicit. First choose $T$ from the terminal estimate. Next choose the stable-field constants, the ordinary failure rate $a$, and the finite diagnostic length for the directional estimate; these choices determine $C_{\mathrm d}$ and $v$. Root stability and the operator bound on $J$ determine the leading weighted-mean constant $C_{\mathrm w}$ independently of selection depth. We may therefore select $\delta$ in <a href="#eq:weighted-bad-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:weighted-bad-bound">[eq:weighted-bad-bound]</a>, then its diagnostic length and $C_\delta$, and finally $n_0$. Every depth and tolerance is fixed before $n$ grows.

With the choices made, the proof needs a finite intersection of matrix events. Include the directional word event and the word events for the selected weighted-mean construction, each with its fixed length and coefficient bounds. Lemma <a href="#lem:words" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:words">2</a> accommodates each fixed $M_0$, and Lemma <a href="#lem:implicit-construction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:implicit-construction">18</a> supplies an actual inverse margin independent of depth. Add the events of Propositions <a href="#prop:stable-fields" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:stable-fields">9</a> and <a href="#prop:terminal-gap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:terminal-gap">27</a>. Their intersection still has probability tending to one, and all constants and the threshold $n_0$ are uniform on it and over the test functions.

For such a matrix and a normalized $f$ with $d_0\le1$, use $V_\tau\le N_\tau$ and the stopped density identity to obtain $$\mathbb E[\mathbf 1_{\mathrm{bad}}V_\tau]
 \le\mathbb E[\mathbf 1_{\mathrm{bad}}N_\tau]
 =\mathbb P^f(\mathrm{bad})\le v/4.$$ On a good path $\tau=T$ and the terminal gap applies, so $$\mathbb E[\mathbf 1_{\mathrm{good}}V_\tau]
 \le2\mathbb E\mathcal D_{Y(T)}(f)\le2d_0.$$ Combining these estimates with <a href="#eq:variance-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:variance-lower">[eq:variance-lower]</a> gives $$v-(1-v)d_0\le2d_0+v/4,
 \qquad\text{hence}\qquad
 d_0\ge\frac{3v}{4(3-v)}\ge\frac v4.$$ The case $d_0>1$ already satisfies this bound. Center and rescale any nonconstant function to obtain Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> once the three inputs have been proved, with the finite constant $C_\beta=4e^{C_{\mathrm d}T}$. We do not optimize this constant. For the clock conversion, the self-adjoint operator $H=\sum_i(I-P_i^0)$ has form $\mathcal D_0$, and one discrete attempt is $I-H/n$. Its gap is therefore the asserted unscaled gap divided by $n$.

<a id="sec:matrices"></a>
### Uniform random-matrix estimates

The field analysis uses random-matrix estimates in two forms. Finite field recursions produce products whose diagonal coefficients may be chosen after the disorder is observed. Lemma <a href="#lem:words" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:words">2</a> controls such products of bounded length, subject to a separate positivity margin whenever an inverse occurs. We also obtain fixed-vector bilinear estimates and a simultaneous determinant bound for the stability argument in Section <a href="#sec:stability" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:stability">5</a>. Their expectation calculations use the same inverse identity. We begin with fixed deterministic coefficients and then use concentration and finite meshes to obtain the required simultaneous estimates.

<a id="eq:matrix-subcritical"></a>
<a id="eq:K"></a>
<a id="eq:K-symmetric-representation"></a>
Throughout this section, $j\in(0,1)$ is fixed. A centered GOE matrix $W$ has independent entries on and above the diagonal, with $$\mathbb EW_{ab}^{2}=j/n\quad(a<b),\qquad
 \mathbb EW_{aa}^{2}=2j/n.$$ We couple $J$ to $W$ by putting $J=W-D_{\mathop{\mathrm{diag}}W}$. In particular, the off-diagonal law of $J$ is the law used in the theorem. Fix $\eta>0$ so that, with $a_+=1+\eta$, $$\label{eq:matrix-subcritical}
 j a_+^2<1.$$ For a nonnegative diagonal matrix $A\le a_+I$, put $$\label{eq:K}
 \begin{gathered}
 \chi_A=\frac1n\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}A,\qquad B_A=j\chi_A,\qquad
 K_A(M)=K_A(1;M),\\
 K_A(z;M)=(I-zAM+z^2B_AA)^{-1}.
 \end{gathered}$$ The associated symmetric matrix is $$S_A(z;M)=I+z^2B_AA-zA^{1/2}MA^{1/2}.$$ The symmetric matrix lets us control the inverse without imposing a positive lower bound on the individual diagonal entries of $A$. Whenever $S_A(z;M)$ is invertible, the following identities hold: <a id="eq:KA-symmetric-representation"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 K_A(z;M)
 &=I+A^{1/2}S_A(z;M)^{-1}A^{1/2}(zM-z^2B_AI),
 \label{eq:K-symmetric-representation}\\
 K_A(z;M)A&=A^{1/2}S_A(z;M)^{-1}A^{1/2}.
 \label{eq:KA-symmetric-representation}
\end{aligned}$$ To verify the first identity, apply $(I-UV)^{-1}=I+U(I-VU)^{-1}V$. For the second, multiply the two sides using $S_A=I-A^{1/2}(zM-z^2B_AI)A^{1/2}$. These formulas will also let us truncate the inverse while retaining bounded matrix factors.

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
#### Words and their predictions

A *word* is an ordered product of diagonal matrices, copies of a symmetric matrix $M$, and at most one factor $K_A(M)$. Its length is the number of factors. Consecutive diagonal factors may always be combined, and an absent diagonal factor is interpreted as $I$. To each word $Q$ we associate a diagonal matrix $\mathsf P(Q)$ by the contraction rule below. The estimates will compare the diagonal of $Q$ with this explicitly defined matrix.

<a id="eq:noncrossing-contraction"></a>
Begin with a word without $K_A$. Consider every complete noncrossing pairing of its occurrences of $M$, in their linear order. For each pairing, remove an innermost pair by the rule $$\label{eq:noncrossing-contraction}
 MDM\ \longmapsto\ j\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}D}{n}I,$$ where $D$ is diagonal after previous removals. Combine adjacent diagonals and continue. Sum the resulting diagonal matrices over all complete noncrossing pairings. If there is no complete pairing, the prediction is zero; if there is no occurrence of $M$, it is the word itself. The result for a fixed pairing does not depend on the order of removal: two available innermost pairs are disjoint, and their removals commute. For words without an inverse factor, this is the operator-valued semicircular moment rule with covariance map $D\mapsto jn^{-1}\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}(D)I$; see Speicher <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[Speicher1998](#ref-Speicher1998)].

For example, four occurrences of $M$ have two complete noncrossing pairings, giving $$\begin{split}
 \mathsf P(MD_1MD_2MD_3M)
 ={}&j^2\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}D_1}{n}\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}D_3}{n}D_2\\
 &+j^2\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}D_2}{n}\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}(D_1D_3)}n I.
 \end{split}$$ The first term pairs the first two and last two occurrences; the second pairs the outside occurrences around the middle pair.

<a id="eq:K-formal-series"></a>
For a word containing $K_A$, replace that factor by the formal series $$\label{eq:K-formal-series}
 K_A(z;M)=\sum_{r\ge0}(zAM-z^2B_AA)^r,$$ and apply the preceding rule coefficient by coefficient. Ordinary occurrences of $M$ outside this factor are left unscaled. We prove below that the resulting series $\mathsf P(Q;z)$ is a polynomial; we define $\mathsf P(Q)=\mathsf P(Q;1)$.

The corresponding trace prediction is $\mathsf p(Q)=n^{-1}\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\mathsf P(Q)$. To see its cyclic invariance, draw an ordinary trace word on a circle, with a chord for each pair in a noncrossing pairing. Its contribution is $j$ for each chord, multiplied by, for each face, the average of the product of the diagonal matrices on that face’s boundary arcs. Removing an innermost chord gives exactly <a href="#eq:noncrossing-contraction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:noncrossing-contraction">[eq:noncrossing-contraction]</a>, and the average over the last face is the final normalized trace. This description is unchanged by rotating the circle. For words with $K_A(z;M)$, apply this argument coefficient by coefficient in <a href="#eq:K-formal-series" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:K-formal-series">[eq:K-formal-series]</a>.

<a id="stmt-lem-words"></a>
<div id="lem:words" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:word-estimates"></a>
**Lemma 2** (Simultaneous word estimates). *Fix $L\in\mathbb N$, $M_0<\infty$, and $c>0$. There are constants $C,c_1>0$ and $n_0$, depending only on $j,a_+,L,M_0,c$, such that the following event has probability at least $1-Ce^{-c_1n}$ for $n\ge n_0$. Simultaneously for all words $Q$ of length at most $L$, all their real diagonal factors of norm at most $M_0$, and all $0\le A\le a_+I$, one has $$\label{eq:word-estimates}
 \lVert Q\rVert\le C,\qquad
 \left(\sum_{a\ne b}|Q_{ab}|^4\right)^{1/4}\le C,\qquad
 \lVert \mathop{\mathrm{diag}}(Q-\mathsf P(Q))\rVert_2\le C.$$ For a word containing $K_A(J)$, the assertion is restricted to parameters satisfying $S_A(1;J)\succeq cI$. The same conclusion holds with $W$ in place of $J$, with the corresponding stability restriction. Thus all diagonal parameters in this assertion may be chosen after the random matrix has been observed.*

*If a word has $r$ ordinary occurrences of $M$ outside its one $K_A(z;M)$ factor, then $\mathsf P(Q;z)$ has degree at most $r$.*

</div>

We prove the simultaneous estimate in three steps. For a fixed deterministic diagonal, Gaussian comparison gives stability along $0\le z\le1$. We then calculate the expected diagonal of a truncated word. Finally, concentration makes the word estimates simultaneous in the diagonal parameters. The first step supplies stability for each deterministic choice; it does not remove the separate stability restriction for adaptive $A$ in Lemma <a href="#lem:words" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:words">2</a>.

<a id="stmt-lem-matrix-path-stability"></a>
<div id="lem:matrix-path-stability" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:deterministic-path-stability"></a>
**Lemma 3** (Stability for a deterministic diagonal). *There is $c_0>0$, depending only on $j,a_+$, with the following property. For every fixed deterministic $0\le A\le a_+I$, and every fixed $R>2\sqrt j$, there are constants $C,c_2>0$ and $n_0$, uniform in $A$, such that $$\label{eq:deterministic-path-stability}
 \mathbb P\left\{\lVert W\rVert\le R\ \hbox{and}\quad
 S_A(z;W)\succeq c_0I\text{ for all }0\le z\le1\right\}
 \ge1-Ce^{-c_2n}\qquad(n\ge n_0).$$ In addition, for every fixed $\varepsilon>0$, $\mathbb P\{\lVert W\rVert>2\sqrt j+\varepsilon\}\le
C_\varepsilon e^{-c_\varepsilon n}$ for sufficiently large $n$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-matrix-path-stability"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-matrix-path-stability</summary>

*Proof.* We first bound the expected quadratic process that defines the smallest eigenvalue of $S_A$. Concentration will give a margin at each fixed $z$, and a finite mesh will extend it along the path.

We use two standard Gaussian facts. A real-valued $L$-Lipschitz function of a standard Gaussian vector satisfies $\mathbb P\{|F-\mathbb EF|>u\}\le2\exp(-u^2/(2L^2))$. Also, if two centered Gaussian processes have ordered squared increments, the process with larger squared increments has the larger expected supremum (Sudakov–Fernique). The latter comparison remains valid after the same deterministic function is added to both processes; see the deterministic-shift comparison in Vitale <a id="pm-cite-27"></a>[[Vitale2000](#ref-Vitale2000)]. Both facts apply by finite nets and continuity to the compact index sets below. The linear map from the independent standard Gaussian coordinates to $W$, in Frobenius norm, has norm $\sqrt{2j/n}$.

<a id="eq:quadratic-process-margin"></a>
For the comparison, write $w=A^{1/2}p$ for $\lVert p\rVert=1$ and consider $$X_p=w^TWw,\qquad
 Y_p=2\sqrt{j/n}\,\lVert w\rVert\,g^Tw,$$ where $g$ is standard Gaussian in $\mathbb R^n$. If $w'=A^{1/2}p'$, direct calculation gives $$\begin{aligned}
 &\mathbb E(Y_p-Y_{p'})^2-\mathbb E(X_p-X_{p'})^2\\
 &\qquad=\frac{2j}{n}
 \left[(\lVert w\rVert^2-\lVert w'\rVert^2)^2
       +2(\lVert w\rVert\lVert w'\rVert-w^Tw')^2\right]\ge0.
\end{aligned}$$ Consequently, for each $z\in[0,1]$, $$\mathbb E\sup_{\lVert p\rVert=1}\{zX_p-z^2j\chi_A\lVert w\rVert^2\}
 \le \mathbb E\sup_{\lVert p\rVert=1}\{zY_p-z^2j\chi_A\lVert w\rVert^2\}.$$ Set $T_g=\sqrt{j/n}\lVert A^{1/2}g\rVert$. Since $g^Tw=p^TA^{1/2}g\le\lVert A^{1/2}g\rVert$, the last supremum is at most $$\sup_{0\le v\le\sqrt{a_+}}
       (2zT_gv-z^2j\chi_Av^2).$$ The remaining supremum depends on the Gaussian vector only through $T_g$. The variable $T_g^2$ has mean $j\chi_A$ and variance $2j^2\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}(A^2)/n^2\le C/n$. The inequality $|\sqrt x-\sqrt y|\le\sqrt{|x-y|}$ therefore shows, uniformly in $A$, that $\mathbb E|T_g-\sqrt{j\chi_A}|=o(1)$. On replacing $T_g$ by $\sqrt{j\chi_A}$ in the preceding display, the expression becomes $2d-d^2$, with $0\le d=z\sqrt{j\chi_A}v\le\sqrt j\,a_+<1$. Thus, with $\delta=(1-\sqrt j\,a_+)^2>0$, $$\label{eq:quadratic-process-margin}
 \mathbb E\sup_{\lVert p\rVert=1}
 \{zp^TA^{1/2}WA^{1/2}p-z^2j\chi_Ap^TAp\}
 \le1-\delta+o(1),$$ uniformly in $A,z$. The supremum in <a href="#eq:quadratic-process-margin" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:quadratic-process-margin">[eq:quadratic-process-margin]</a> is $a_+$-Lipschitz in $W$ for Frobenius norm. Gaussian concentration gives an exponentially small probability that it exceeds $1-\delta/2$, uniformly for each fixed $A,z$.

To pass from a fixed $z$ to the entire interval, we also need a bound on $\lVert W\rVert$. Taking $A=I$ in the comparison without deterministic offsets gives $$\mathbb E\lambda_{\max}(W)\le2\sqrt{j/n}\mathbb E\lVert g\rVert\le2\sqrt j.$$ The same holds for $-W$. Concentration of these two eigenvalues proves the stated norm tail. On $\lVert W\rVert\le R$, the function of $z$ inside the supremum in <a href="#eq:quadratic-process-margin" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:quadratic-process-margin">[eq:quadratic-process-margin]</a> is uniformly Lipschitz, with constant at most $a_+R+2ja_+^2$. A sufficiently fine constant mesh of $[0,1]$, followed by a union bound, therefore gives <a href="#eq:deterministic-path-stability" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:deterministic-path-stability">[eq:deterministic-path-stability]</a>, for example with $c_0=\delta/4$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-15"></a>
#### Truncation and the loop recursion

The inverse in a word need not exist for every realization of the matrix. We therefore replace each word by a globally defined bounded function of $W$, agreeing with the original word whenever the required norm and stability conditions hold. This permits concentration and integration by parts before those conditions are imposed.

Fix a sufficiently large constant $R$. Let $\Pi_R$ be metric projection, in the Hilbert space of real symmetric matrices with Frobenius norm, onto $\{M:\lVert M\rVert\le R\}$. This is a nonexpansive map and is equivariant under orthogonal conjugation. Put $\widehat W=\Pi_R(W)$. Choose a smooth compactly supported real function $f$ which equals $x^{-1}$ on the interval $$\left[\tfrac12\min(c,c_0),\,2+c+c_0+ja_+^2+a_+R\right].$$ This interval contains, with slack, every stable spectrum needed both for deterministic-parameter estimates and for the restriction $S_A(1;M)\succeq cI$ in Lemma <a href="#lem:words" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:words">2</a>. Replace $K_A(z;W)$ by <a id="eq:truncated-K"></a>$$\label{eq:truncated-K}
 \widehat K_A(z)
 =I+A^{1/2}f(S_A(z;\widehat W))A^{1/2}
       (z\widehat W-z^2B_AI).$$ Replace every ordinary $W$ in a word by $\widehat W$ as well; denote the resulting word by $\widehat Q_\theta(z)$, where $\theta$ lists all its diagonal parameters. These truncated factors equal the original factors on the stability events where they will be used.

<a id="eq:truncated-word-lipschitz"></a>
<a id="eq:truncated-word-increment-lipschitz"></a>
For each fixed length and fixed bounds on the diagonal parameters, there is a constant $C$ such that $$\begin{aligned}
 \lVert \widehat Q_\theta(z)\rVert&\le C,
 &\lVert \widehat Q_\theta(z;W)-\widehat Q_\theta(z;W')\rVert_{\mathrm F}
 &\le C\lVert W-W'\rVert_{\mathrm F},
 \label{eq:truncated-word-lipschitz}\\
 \mathop{\mathrm{Lip}}_{\mathrm F}\bigl(\widehat Q_\theta(z;\cdot)
                   -\widehat Q_{\theta'}(z;\cdot)\bigr)
 &\le C\sqrt\Delta
 &&\text{if }\lVert \theta-\theta'\rVert_\infty\le\Delta\le1.
 \label{eq:truncated-word-increment-lipschitz}
\end{aligned}$$ The constants are uniform for $z\in[0,1]$ and for all matrix sizes. Here the Lipschitz constants concern maps into matrices with Frobenius norm.

We verify both estimates with constants independent of dimension. On the operator-norm ball, all factors are bounded, and their differentials in a perturbation $E$ are bounded in Frobenius norm by $C\lVert E\rVert_{\mathrm F}$. The only functional-calculus factor is handled by writing $f$ as a Fourier integral and using $$\mathrm d(e^{itS})[E]
 =it\int_0^1 e^{it(1-u)S}E e^{ituS}\,\mathrm du.$$ The Fourier transform of $f$ decays faster than any power, so its first two weighted absolute moments are finite. This formula and the corresponding difference formula give $$\lVert \mathrm df(S)[E]\rVert_{\mathrm F}\le C\lVert E\rVert_{\mathrm F},\qquad
 \lVert (\mathrm df(S)-\mathrm df(S'))[E]\rVert_{\mathrm F}
 \le C\lVert S-S'\rVert\lVert E\rVert_{\mathrm F}.$$ Changing the diagonal parameters by at most $\Delta$ changes their square roots in norm by at most $\sqrt\Delta$. Consequently the factors, and their matrix differentials, change by respectively $C\sqrt\Delta$ and $C\sqrt\Delta\lVert E\rVert_{\mathrm F}$. The product rule proves the same differential assertions for a word. Integrating along the line segment between two matrices in the convex operator-norm ball proves <a href="#eq:truncated-word-lipschitz" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:truncated-word-lipschitz">[eq:truncated-word-lipschitz]</a> and <a href="#eq:truncated-word-increment-lipschitz" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:truncated-word-increment-lipschitz">[eq:truncated-word-increment-lipschitz]</a> there. Composition with the nonexpansive projection $\Pi_R$ proves them everywhere.

<a id="eq:word-entry-and-trace-variance"></a>
These Lipschitz estimates first give the moment bounds needed for the expectation calculation. For a fixed parameter tuple, $$\label{eq:word-entry-and-trace-variance}
 \mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(\widehat Q_{ab})\le C/n,\qquad
 \mathop{\mathrm{Var}}\left(\frac1n\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\widehat Q\right)\le C/n^2.$$ The normalized trace gains a factor $n^{-1/2}$ in its Frobenius Lipschitz constant. The same estimates for a difference of two parameter tuples gain a factor $\Delta$ on the right-hand sides. Fourth centered entry moments are bounded by $C/n^2$, or $C\Delta^2/n^2$ for such a difference, by integrating the Gaussian concentration tail.

<a id="stmt-lem-word-bias"></a>
<div id="lem:word-bias" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:word-bias"></a>
**Lemma 4** (Fixed-parameter diagonal expectations). *For the truncated words just defined, uniformly in deterministic diagonal parameters in the indicated bounded ranges, in $z\in[0,1]$, and in the diagonal index $i$, $$\label{eq:word-bias}
 \mathbb E\widehat Q_\theta(z)_{ii}
 =\mathsf P(Q_\theta;z)_{ii}+O(n^{-1}).$$ For words without $K_A$, the prediction has no $z$ dependence. The formal prediction for a word with $r$ ordinary $W$ letters is a polynomial of degree at most $r$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-word-bias"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-word-bias</summary>

*Proof.* The calculation has two parts. We first justify integration by parts for the truncated words and compute the inverse factor alone. We then obtain a recursion in the number of ordinary $W$ factors; the same recursion determines the formal prediction exactly and the expectation up to $O(n^{-1})$.

<a id="eq:GOE-integration-by-parts"></a>
All constants in this proof depend only on the fixed length and parameter bounds. Lemma <a href="#lem:matrix-path-stability" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:matrix-path-stability">3</a> shows that, for every fixed deterministic parameter tuple, the truncations agree with the actual matrices for all $z\in[0,1]$, except on an event of probability $Ce^{-c_2n}$. Gaussian integration by parts can be applied to the globally Lipschitz truncated factors, using weak derivatives. More explicitly, for a scalar Lipschitz function $H$, $$\label{eq:GOE-integration-by-parts}
 \mathbb E[W_{ab}H(W)]
 =\frac jn\mathbb E\bigl[\mathrm dH(W)[E_{ab}+E_{ba}]\bigr].$$ When $a=b$, the elementary direction on the right is doubled, as required by the diagonal variance $2j/n$.

For <a href="#eq:GOE-integration-by-parts" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:GOE-integration-by-parts">[eq:GOE-integration-by-parts]</a>, remove the projection from the distinguished ordinary $W$ factor. The resulting expectation error is exponentially small, since the other factors are bounded and Gaussian norm tails control the removed factor. On the event of agreement, the differential of the inverse is <a id="eq:inverse-differential"></a>$$\label{eq:inverse-differential}
 \mathrm dK_A(z;W)[E]=zK_A(z;W)AEK_A(z;W).$$ We may use the expressions on the right, with truncated factors, on the whole probability space. The error is exponentially small even after the index sums: all factors and their true truncated differentials are bounded, there are at most a fixed polynomial number of indices, and the disagreement event has exponentially small probability. More precisely, the elementary direction $E_{ab}+E_{ba}$ has Frobenius norm at most $2$. Both the true weak differential and its formal replacement are therefore bounded by $C$ in each entry; their difference vanishes on the agreement event. Each such differential error has expected absolute value at most $Ce^{-c_2n}$. Expanding the diagonal entry of a length-$L$ word and applying the product rule uses at most $(L-1)n^{L-1}$ scalar contributions before any index identifications; the factor $j/n$ in integration by parts only improves this bound. Thus their total error is at most a fixed polynomial in $n$ times $e^{-c_2n}$, hence at most $C'e^{-c'n}$. Subsequent words have bounded lengths depending only on the original length. We therefore suppress hats during the loop calculation, with this convention understood.

<a id="eq:K-loop-equations"></a>
We now compute the base case, in which the only nondiagonal factor is $K=K_A(z;W)$. Write $a_i=A_{ii}$, $k_i(z)=\mathbb EK_{ii}$, and $r(z)=n^{-1}\sum_i a_i k_i(z)$. Differentiating $K_{bi}$ in <a href="#eq:GOE-integration-by-parts" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:GOE-integration-by-parts">[eq:GOE-integration-by-parts]</a> gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E(WK)_{ii}
 &=\frac{zj}{n}\mathbb E\left[(\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}KA)K_{ii}\right]
   +\frac{zj}{n}\sum_b\mathbb E[(KA)_{bi}K_{bi}]+O(e^{-c'n})\\
 &=zjr(z)k_i(z)+O(n^{-1}).
\end{aligned}$$ For the second sum, the absolute value of the sum over $b$ is at most $\lVert KAe_i\rVert\lVert Ke_i\rVert\le C$. The factorization in the first term follows from <a href="#eq:word-entry-and-trace-variance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:word-entry-and-trace-variance">[eq:word-entry-and-trace-variance]</a>; its covariance is at most $C n^{-3/2}$, which is more than enough. The inverse identity $K=I+zAWK-z^2B_AAK$ now yields $$\label{eq:K-loop-equations}
 \mathbb E(WK)_{ii}=zjr k_i+O(n^{-1}),\qquad
 k_i=1+z^2ja_i(r-\chi_A)k_i+O(n^{-1}).$$

<a id="eq:K-and-WK-bias"></a>
The loop equations must select the solution near $k_i=1$. We establish this by continuation from $z=0$, where the inverse is the identity. Put $h(z)=r(z)-\chi_A$. The truncated $k_i$ are uniformly bounded and continuous in $z$. If $|h(z)|\le\delta_1$, the second equation in <a href="#eq:K-loop-equations" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:K-loop-equations">[eq:K-loop-equations]</a> gives $\max_i|k_i(z)-1|\le C\delta_1+C/n$. Choose $\delta_1>0$ small, using <a href="#eq:matrix-subcritical" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:matrix-subcritical">[eq:matrix-subcritical]</a>, so that throughout this region and for large $n$, $$\left|z^2j\frac1n\sum_i a_i^2 k_i(z)\right|
 \le1-\delta_2$$ for some fixed $\delta_2>0$. Averaging the second equation in <a href="#eq:K-loop-equations" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:K-loop-equations">[eq:K-loop-equations]</a> with weights $a_i$ gives $$h(z)=z^2j\left(\frac1n\sum_i a_i^2k_i(z)\right)h(z)+O(n^{-1}),$$ and hence $|h(z)|\le C/n$ while $|h(z)|\le\delta_1$. Since $h(0)=0$, continuity rules out a first exit from that region for large $n$. It follows uniformly in $z$ that $$\label{eq:K-and-WK-bias}
 \mathbb EK_{ii}=1+O(n^{-1}),\qquad
 \mathbb E(WK)_{ii}=zB_A+O(n^{-1}).$$

To complete the base case, we verify the formal identity $\mathsf P(K_A(z;W))=I$ independently of the expectation calculation. The cancellation takes place at each coefficient of the series. In the expansion <a href="#eq:K-formal-series" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:K-formal-series">[eq:K-formal-series]</a>, a block $-z^2B_AA$ may be regarded as the negative contraction of two adjacent blocks $zAW,zAW$, since their contraction is $z^2j\chi_AA=z^2B_AA$. Fix a completely noncrossing-paired chain $(AW)^{2m}$ with $m>0$. Let $\mathcal A$ be the nonempty set of its pairs which join two originally adjacent occurrences of $W$. It is nonempty because every nonempty noncrossing pairing of a linear order has an adjacent pair. All pairs in $\mathcal A$ are disjoint. The terms of <a href="#eq:K-formal-series" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:K-formal-series">[eq:K-formal-series]</a> which give this paired chain are in bijection with subsets $T\subseteq\mathcal A$: contract the pairs in $T$ into the negative deterministic blocks, and pair the remaining occurrences normally. The magnitude of the final diagonal contribution is unchanged, and its sign is $(-1)^{|T|}$. Thus the total multiplier is $\sum_{T\subseteq\mathcal A}(-1)^{|T|}=0$. This argument is finite at each power of $z$ and proves the asserted formal identity. Multiplication by surrounding diagonal matrices proves both claims of the lemma for words with no ordinary $W$ letters.

<a id="eq:word-loop-contractions"></a>
<a id="eq:word-loop-leading"></a>
For the induction step, select an ordinary occurrence of $W$ in a general word and apply integration by parts at that occurrence. We describe the resulting finite recursion directly, without requiring convergence of the formal series. In each product-rule term, mark the partner of the selected occurrence: for a derivative falling on an ordinary factor, this is the $W$ being differentiated. If the derivative falls on $K_A$, replace that factor by $zK_AA\,\boxed W\,K_A$ and take the boxed occurrence as the partner. In either case, list the two marked positions in their linear order, so the marked product reads $$U\,\boxed W\,V\,\boxed W\,Q.$$ The two orientations in <a href="#eq:GOE-integration-by-parts" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:GOE-integration-by-parts">[eq:GOE-integration-by-parts]</a> contribute respectively $$\label{eq:word-loop-contractions}
 \frac jn\mathbb E\bigl[(UQ)_{ii}\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}V\bigr],\qquad
 \frac jn\mathbb E(UV^TQ)_{ii}.$$ There is an additional factor $z$ when the derivative fell on $K_A$. The second expression in <a href="#eq:word-loop-contractions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:word-loop-contractions">[eq:word-loop-contractions]</a> is $O(n^{-1})$ by operator-norm bounds, including when it contains two copies of $K_A$. The first expression is $$\label{eq:word-loop-leading}
 j\left(\frac1n\mathbb E\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}V\right)\mathbb E(UQ)_{ii}+O(n^{-1}),$$ again by <a href="#eq:word-entry-and-trace-variance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:word-entry-and-trace-variance">[eq:word-entry-and-trace-variance]</a>. The index sums explain the two terms. The opposite orientation identifies the two interior indices and gives $\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}V$; the equal orientation reverses the interior path and gives $V^T$.

Consequently $\mathbb E\widehat Q_{ii}$ is the finite sum of the leading products <a href="#eq:word-loop-leading" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:word-loop-leading">[eq:word-loop-leading]</a> over all ordinary partners, plus $z$ times the corresponding leading product for the one $K_A$ factor if present, with an $O(n^{-1})$ remainder. Each word $V$ and $UQ$ in this recursion has fewer ordinary $W$ letters than the original word. Each contains at most one $K_A$: if the derivative fell on $K_A$, the splitting places one of its two copies in each word. Extra diagonal factors $A$ cause no problem, since at most one is added per decrease of the induction index.

The decrease in the number of ordinary letters holds whether the selected occurrence lies before or after the inverse. The two possible orders can be checked explicitly. If the original word is $P K_A R W T$, the contraction with $K_A$ contributes $$zj\,\mathbb E\left[(P K_A A T)_{ii}\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}(K_A R)}n\right].$$ If it is $P W R K_A T$, that contribution is $$zj\,\mathbb E\left[(P K_A T)_{ii}\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}(R K_A A)}n\right].$$ Here $P,R,T$ contain no inverse factor. The displayed outer and inner words each have one inverse factor, even though the original distinguished occurrence may lie anywhere among the ordinary letters.

It remains to compare this expectation recursion with the formal prediction. Sort the noncrossing pairings by the partner of the selected ordinary occurrence. The inside and outside may then be paired independently; their predictions are respectively $\mathsf p(V)$ and $\mathsf P(UQ)$. If the partner occurs inside the series for $K_A(z;W)$, marking that occurrence in the formal series is exactly the formal differentiation identity $\mathrm dK_A[E]=zK_AAEK_A$. Thus the entire sum over positions in that series is the single $z$-weighted product just described.

We can now induct on the number $r$ of ordinary $W$ letters. An ordinary partner leaves a total of $r-2$ such letters in the two smaller words; a partner in $K_A$ leaves $r-1$. The base prediction is constant. The recursion therefore proves that the formal prediction is a polynomial of degree at most $r$. It also proves <a href="#eq:word-bias" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:word-bias">[eq:word-bias]</a>: use the induction hypothesis for the two expectations in each leading product, and average the diagonal induction hypothesis to handle its normalized trace. There are only finitely many products and all factors are uniformly bounded. The same induction, with no inverse factor, proves the statement for ordinary words. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-16"></a>
#### Making the word estimates simultaneous

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-word-bias-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-word-bias</summary>

*Proof of Lemma <a href="#lem:words" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:words">2</a>.* We first make the centered estimates uniform over the diagonal parameters, then add the expectation estimate from Lemma <a href="#lem:word-bias" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:word-bias">4</a>. Fix a letter pattern and let $\theta$ range over its parameter box, which has at most $C_Ln$ real coordinates. Work initially with the truncated words at $z=1$. Conjugation by any diagonal sign matrix preserves the law of $W$ and conjugates $\widehat Q_\theta$ by that same sign matrix. The diagonal parameters commute with the conjugation, and both the projection and the functional calculus respect it. Thus $\mathbb E(\widehat Q_\theta)_{ab}=0$ whenever $a\ne b$.

<a id="eq:centered-word-norm-expectations"></a>
Define the two matrix seminorms $$N_2(M)=\lVert \mathop{\mathrm{diag}}M\rVert_2,\qquad
 N_4(M)=\left(\sum_{a\ne b}|M_{ab}|^4\right)^{1/4}.$$ Both are $1$-Lipschitz with respect to Frobenius norm. The entry moment bounds following <a href="#eq:word-entry-and-trace-variance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:word-entry-and-trace-variance">[eq:word-entry-and-trace-variance]</a> give $$\label{eq:centered-word-norm-expectations}
 \mathbb EN_k(\widehat Q_\theta-\mathbb E\widehat Q_\theta)\le C
 \quad(k=2,4).$$ Indeed, sum the $n$ second moments for $k=2$, and the at most $n^2$ fourth moments for $k=4$, then apply Jensen’s inequality. For two parameter tuples at distance at most $\Delta$, the same argument, using <a href="#eq:truncated-word-increment-lipschitz" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:truncated-word-increment-lipschitz">[eq:truncated-word-increment-lipschitz]</a>, gives $$\mathbb EN_k\bigl((\widehat Q_\theta-\mathbb E\widehat Q_\theta)
             -(\widehat Q_{\theta'}-\mathbb E\widehat Q_{\theta'})\bigr)
 \le C\sqrt\Delta.$$ The random norms in this last display concentrate around their means at Gaussian scale $C\sqrt{\Delta/n}$, again by <a href="#eq:truncated-word-increment-lipschitz" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:truncated-word-increment-lipschitz">[eq:truncated-word-increment-lipschitz]</a>. The centered expectations are deterministic and do not change Lipschitz constants.

<a id="eq:simultaneous-centered-words"></a>
To control every parameter tuple, approximate it successively on finer meshes. For each $\ell\ge0$, choose a sup-norm mesh $\mathcal N_\ell$ of spacing $2^{-\ell}$ in the parameter box. It may be chosen with $$|\mathcal N_\ell|\le\exp(C_L(\ell+1)n).$$ For a tuple $\theta$, choose nearest mesh points $\theta_\ell\in\mathcal N_\ell$. Then $\lVert \theta_\ell-\theta_{\ell-1}\rVert_\infty\le C2^{-\ell}$. For all pairs of mesh points at this distance, concentration and a union bound show that their centered increments have both $N_2$ and $N_4$ norm at most $$C'\sqrt{\ell+1}\,2^{-\ell/2},$$ except with probability $Ce^{-c(\ell+1)n}$, provided $C'$ is chosen sufficiently large. Specifically, the squared ratio of this threshold to the Gaussian scale is a constant multiple of $(\ell+1)n$, and its constant can be chosen to dominate the logarithm of the number of mesh pairs. The initial mesh is bounded in the same way using <a href="#eq:centered-word-norm-expectations" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:centered-word-norm-expectations">[eq:centered-word-norm-expectations]</a>. Summing the failure probabilities over $\ell$, and summing the convergent increment bounds along each chain, proves, with probability $1-Ce^{-c_1n}$, $$\label{eq:simultaneous-centered-words}
 \sup_\theta N_k(\widehat Q_\theta-\mathbb E\widehat Q_\theta)\le C
 \quad(k=2,4).$$ At each fixed $n$, continuity of the truncated word and its expectation in $\theta$ justifies taking the limit of the mesh chain. There are only finitely many letter patterns of length at most $L$, so their events may be intersected.

The centered words are now controlled on one event for every parameter tuple. Lemma <a href="#lem:word-bias" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:word-bias">4</a> supplies the remaining bias estimate, $N_2(\mathbb E\widehat Q_\theta-\mathsf P(Q_\theta))\le C/\sqrt n$, uniformly in $\theta$. Sign symmetry makes the off-diagonal of $\mathbb E\widehat Q_\theta$ identically zero. Combining these facts with <a href="#eq:simultaneous-centered-words" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:simultaneous-centered-words">[eq:simultaneous-centered-words]</a> proves the desired diagonal and off-diagonal assertions for the truncated words. Their operator norms are already bounded by construction. On $\lVert W\rVert\le R$ and at parameters with $S_A(1;W)\succeq cI$, the truncations equal the exact words, proving the GOE version.

The GOE estimate transfers to the zero-diagonal model through $J=W-D_{\mathop{\mathrm{diag}}W}$. This last step uses a Frobenius bound on the removed diagonal. Since $$\lVert D_{\mathop{\mathrm{diag}}W}\rVert_{\mathrm F}^{2}
   \ \stackrel{\mathrm{law}}=\ \frac{2j}{n}\chi_n^2,$$ it is bounded by a fixed constant except with exponentially small probability. The norm tail for $W$, and a sufficiently large choice of $R$, ensure also that both $W$ and $J$ lie in the operator-norm ball except with exponentially small probability. For every parameter tuple, <a href="#eq:truncated-word-lipschitz" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:truncated-word-lipschitz">[eq:truncated-word-lipschitz]</a> then bounds the Frobenius norm of the difference of the truncated word evaluated at $W$ and at $J$ by a fixed constant. Both $N_2$ and $N_4$ of this difference are bounded by its Frobenius norm. The prediction is unchanged by this substitution. At parameters with $S_A(1;J)\succeq cI$, the truncated factors evaluated at $J$ equal the exact factors. This proves all of <a href="#eq:word-estimates" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:word-estimates">[eq:word-estimates]</a>. The polynomial assertion was proved in Lemma <a href="#lem:word-bias" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:word-bias">4</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
#### Bilinear forms and regularized determinants

We next extract two forms of the preceding estimates. The first controls bilinear forms for a prescribed finite family of deterministic vectors. The second controls a regularized determinant simultaneously over its diagonal parameters and bounded finite-rank perturbations. Their quantifiers differ, and we keep the two statements separate.

<a id="stmt-lem-bilinear"></a>
<div id="lem:bilinear" class="lemma">

**Lemma 5** (Bilinear deterministic equivalents). *Fix an integer $m\ge1$, a vector bound $M_1<\infty$, a tolerance $\varepsilon>0$, and $R>2\sqrt j$. For every deterministic $0\le A\le a_+I$ and deterministic vectors $v_1,\ldots,v_m\in\mathbb R^n$ with $\lVert v_r\rVert\le M_1$, there is an event of probability at least $1-Ce^{-c_3n}$ on which <a href="#eq:deterministic-path-stability" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:deterministic-path-stability">[eq:deterministic-path-stability]</a> holds and, with $$G_A=A^{1/2}S_A(1;W)^{-1}A^{1/2}=K_A(W)A,$$ one has, for every $r,s$, <a id="eq:bilinear-G"></a><a id="eq:bilinear-GW"></a><a id="eq:bilinear-WGW"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 |v_r^T(G_A-A)v_s|&\le\varepsilon,
 \label{eq:bilinear-G}\\
 |v_r^T(G_AW-B_AA)v_s|&\le\varepsilon,
 \label{eq:bilinear-GW}\\
 |v_r^T(WG_AW-B_AI-B_A^2A)v_s|&\le\varepsilon.
 \label{eq:bilinear-WGW}
\end{aligned}$$ Here $C,c_3,n_0$ may depend on $j,a_+,m,M_1,\varepsilon,R$ but are uniform in $A$, the vectors, and $n\ge n_0$. The assertion is for each deterministic choice of these parameters; it does not claim simultaneity over all vector choices.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-bilinear"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-bilinear</summary>

*Proof.* For each of the specified bilinear forms, we calculate its expectation and then apply concentration. Use the truncations above with a cutoff agreeing with the inverse on the path-stability event. Sign conjugation makes the off-diagonal expectations of each relevant matrix vanish. Equation <a href="#eq:K-and-WK-bias" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:K-and-WK-bias">[eq:K-and-WK-bias]</a> at $z=1$ gives $$\mathbb E\widehat K_{ii}=1+O(n^{-1}),\qquad
 \mathbb E(\widehat W\widehat K)_{ii}=B_A+O(n^{-1}).$$ The identities, valid on the event of agreement, $$G_AW=K_A(W)(I+B_AA)-I,
 \qquad
 WG_AW=WK_A(W)(I+B_AA)-W$$ therefore give the three diagonal expectation predictions $A$, $B_AA$, and $B_AI+B_A^2A$, with entrywise errors $O(n^{-1})$. Their use with truncated factors changes expectations only exponentially little, by the same argument as in Lemma <a href="#lem:word-bias" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:word-bias">4</a>. In particular, their errors in any of the specified bilinear forms are $O(n^{-1})$: for a diagonal error matrix $E$, one has $|v_r^TEv_s|\le\lVert E\rVert\lVert v_r\rVert\lVert v_s\rVert$.

The expectation calculation has identified the three required predictions. To control fluctuations, each truncated bilinear form is $C M_1^2$-Lipschitz in $W$ for Frobenius norm, by <a href="#eq:truncated-word-lipschitz" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:truncated-word-lipschitz">[eq:truncated-word-lipschitz]</a>. Gaussian concentration gives failure probability $Ce^{-c_3n}$ at any fixed positive tolerance. Intersect these events for the $3m^2$ forms and with Lemma <a href="#lem:matrix-path-stability" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:matrix-path-stability">3</a>. For large $n$ the expectation errors are less than half the specified tolerance, proving the result. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-logdet"></a>
<div id="lem:logdet" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:logdet-upper-bound"></a>
**Lemma 6** (A simultaneous regularized determinant bound). *Fix $\varepsilon>0$, an integer $r_0\ge0$, and $M_2<\infty$. There is $\gamma_0>0$, depending only on $j,\varepsilon$, such that for every fixed $0<\gamma\le\gamma_0$ and all sufficiently large $n$, an event of probability at least $1-Ce^{-c_4n}$ has the following property. Simultaneously for all diagonal $0\le V\le I$ and all real matrices $E$ with $\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}E\le r_0$ and $\lVert E\rVert\le M_2$, put $$b=\frac1n\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}V,\qquad B=jb,\qquad
 L=I+(BI-W)V+E.$$ Then $$\label{eq:logdet-upper-bound}
 \frac1n\log\det(L^TL+\gamma I)\le jb^2+\varepsilon.$$ The constants $C,c_4$ and the lower threshold on $n$ may depend on $j,\varepsilon,\gamma,r_0,M_2$. In particular, $V$ and $E$ may depend on $W$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-logdet"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-logdet</summary>

<a id="eq:logdet-derivative"></a>
*Proof.* We first compute the mean log determinant with $E=0$ and a fixed deterministic $V$. Regularization will then provide a global Lipschitz bound, allowing a finite mesh in $V$ and finally the finite-rank perturbation. Set $$L_z=I+z^2BV-zWV,
 \qquad K(z)=(I-zVW+z^2BV)^{-1}.$$ Since $V\le I$, we use the proof of Lemma <a href="#lem:matrix-path-stability" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:matrix-path-stability">3</a> with the diagonal upper bound equal to $1$; its stability and inverse-norm constants here depend only on $j$. On the path-stability event from Lemma <a href="#lem:matrix-path-stability" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:matrix-path-stability">3</a> with $A=V$, the determinant of $L_z$ equals the positive determinant of $S_V(z;W)$ by $\det(I-UV)=\det(I-VU)$, and both $L_z^{-1}$ and $K(z)$ have uniformly bounded operator norm. Indeed $L_z=K(z)^{-T}$, and <a href="#eq:K-symmetric-representation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:K-symmetric-representation">[eq:K-symmetric-representation]</a> applies. Differentiating the normalized log determinant on this event gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \frac{\mathrm d}{\mathrm dz}\frac1n\log\det L_z
 &=\frac1n\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\bigl[L_z^{-1}(2zBV-WV)\bigr]\notag\\
 &=2zB\frac1n\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}(K(z)V)
      -\frac1n\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}(WK(z)V).
 \label{eq:logdet-derivative}
\end{aligned}$$ Here $VL_z^{-1}=K(z)V$, which follows by multiplying $(I-zVW+z^2BV)V=V L_z$, and cyclicity of trace was used.

<a id="eq:regularized-logdet-mean"></a>
The derivative is expressed in the two matrices whose diagonal expectations were computed in <a href="#eq:K-and-WK-bias" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:K-and-WK-bias">[eq:K-and-WK-bias]</a>. Let $\mathcal E_V$ be the path-stability event intersected with $\lVert W\rVert\le R$, for a fixed $R>2\sqrt j$. This one event applies to the entire interval of $z$. Equations <a href="#eq:K-and-WK-bias" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:K-and-WK-bias">[eq:K-and-WK-bias]</a>, and replacement by the truncated factors off $\mathcal E_V$, show uniformly in $z,V$ that the expectation of the right-hand side of <a href="#eq:logdet-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:logdet-derivative">[eq:logdet-derivative]</a>, multiplied by $\mathbf 1_{\mathcal E_V}$, is $$2zBb-zBb+O(n^{-1})=zjb^2+O(n^{-1}).$$ All differentiated quantities are bounded on $\mathcal E_V$, so integration and expectation may be interchanged. Since $L_0=I$, it follows that <a id="eq:unregularized-logdet-mean"></a>$$\label{eq:unregularized-logdet-mean}
 \mathbb E\left[\mathbf 1_{\mathcal E_V}\frac2n\log\det L_1\right]
 =jb^2+O(n^{-1}).$$ On $\mathcal E_V$, the singular values of $L_1$ are bounded below by a positive constant. Therefore $$0\le\frac1n\log\det(L_1^TL_1+\gamma I)
       -\frac2n\log\det L_1
 \le C\gamma.$$ Off $\mathcal E_V$, the absolute value of the regularized normalized log determinant is bounded by $$|\log\gamma|+C+2\log(1+\lVert W\rVert),$$ for $0<\gamma\le1$. The norm tails of $W$ and <a href="#eq:deterministic-path-stability" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:deterministic-path-stability">[eq:deterministic-path-stability]</a> make its expected contribution exponentially small, for each fixed $\gamma$. Consequently, uniformly in deterministic $V$, $$\label{eq:regularized-logdet-mean}
 \mathbb E\left[\frac1n\log\det(L_1^TL_1+\gamma I)\right]
 \le jb^2+C\gamma+O(n^{-1})+O_\gamma(e^{-c n}).$$

<a id="eq:regularized-logdet-lipschitz"></a>
<a id="eq:regularized-logdet-concentration"></a>
The mean bound is uniform in deterministic $V$. To obtain a single event for all $V$, we use the concentration supplied by regularization. Define on all real matrices $$F_\gamma(M)=\frac1n\log\det(M^TM+\gamma I).$$ Its Frobenius gradient is $2n^{-1}M(M^TM+\gamma I)^{-1}$. Each singular value of this gradient is at most $1/(n\sqrt\gamma)$, and hence $$\label{eq:regularized-logdet-lipschitz}
 \mathop{\mathrm{Lip}}_{\mathrm F}(F_\gamma)\le\frac1{\sqrt{n\gamma}}.$$ For fixed $V$, the map $W\mapsto L_1$ has Frobenius Lipschitz constant at most one. Gaussian concentration therefore gives $$\label{eq:regularized-logdet-concentration}
 \mathbb P\{|F_\gamma(L_1)-\mathbb EF_\gamma(L_1)|>u\}
 \le2\exp\left(-\frac{\gamma n^2u^2}{4j}\right).$$

Choose $\gamma_0\le1$ small enough that $C\gamma_0$ in <a href="#eq:regularized-logdet-mean" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:regularized-logdet-mean">[eq:regularized-logdet-mean]</a> is at most $\varepsilon/8$. For fixed $\gamma\le\gamma_0$ and sufficiently large $n$, the expectation bound and <a href="#eq:regularized-logdet-concentration" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:regularized-logdet-concentration">[eq:regularized-logdet-concentration]</a> bound all points of any fixed sup-norm mesh of the cube $[0,1]^n$ by $jb^2+\varepsilon/2$, with failure $\exp(-c_\gamma n^2)$. Indeed a mesh of spacing $\Delta>0$ has at most $(C/\Delta)^n$ points, whose logarithm is only of order $n$. On $\lVert W\rVert\le R$, if $\lVert V-V'\rVert\le\Delta$, then $$|b-b'|\le\Delta,\qquad
 \lVert (I+(BI-W)V)-(I+(B'I-W)V')\rVert_{\mathrm F}
 \le\sqrt n(2j+R)\Delta.$$ By <a href="#eq:regularized-logdet-lipschitz" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:regularized-logdet-lipschitz">[eq:regularized-logdet-lipschitz]</a>, the corresponding $F_\gamma$ values differ by at most $(2j+R)\Delta/\sqrt\gamma$, whereas $|jb^2-j(b')^2|\le2j\Delta$. Choose $\Delta$, depending only on $j,R,\gamma,\varepsilon$, so that these errors sum to at most $\varepsilon/4$. Including the exponentially likely norm event proves, simultaneously for all $V$, $$F_\gamma(I+(BI-W)V)\le jb^2+3\varepsilon/4.$$

We have obtained the simultaneous bound for $E=0$ with room for the perturbation. The same Lipschitz estimate <a href="#eq:regularized-logdet-lipschitz" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:regularized-logdet-lipschitz">[eq:regularized-logdet-lipschitz]</a> gives, for every allowed $E$, $$|F_\gamma(M+E)-F_\gamma(M)|
 \le\frac{\lVert E\rVert_{\mathrm F}}{\sqrt{n\gamma}}
 \le M_2\sqrt{\frac{r_0}{n\gamma}}.$$ This is at most $\varepsilon/4$ for large $n$, uniformly even when $E$ is chosen from the observed matrix. It proves <a href="#eq:logdet-upper-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:logdet-upper-bound">[eq:logdet-upper-bound]</a> and the lemma. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:entropy"></a>
### A scalar entropy inequality

The stability argument needs a scalar criterion that forces an empirical field law toward a Gaussian law. We first bound relative entropy below by a squared discrepancy involving the field and its hyperbolic tangent. We then identify the Gaussian law compatible with the scalar consistency equations. Together with the equality case of the first estimate, this characterizes the consistent equality laws.

Throughout this section, $N(d,s)$ denotes the Gaussian law with mean $d$ and *variance* $s$. All scalar expectations are taken with respect to the indicated probability law.

<a id="stmt-lem-scalar-entropy"></a>
<div id="lem:scalar-entropy" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:scalar-entropy"></a>
**Lemma 7** (Scalar entropy inequality). *Let $0<j<1$, $d\in\mathbb R$, and $s>0$. Let $P$ be a probability law on $\mathbb R$ with finite second moment, and set $$m(y)=\tanh y,\qquad q=\mathbb E_P m^2,\qquad b=1-q,\qquad
 a=\mathbb E_P[(y-d)m],\qquad S=s+jq.$$ If $s\ge jq$, then $$D(P\Vert N(d,s))\ge \frac{j(a-sb)^2}{2sS}.
 \label{eq:scalar-entropy}$$ Equality holds if and only if $P=N(d,s)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-scalar-entropy"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-scalar-entropy</summary>

<a id="eq:entropy-target"></a>
*Proof.* The second-moment assumption makes the right-hand side finite, so it suffices to consider the case $D:=D(P\Vert N(d,s))<\infty$. In this case $P$ is absolutely continuous, and hence $q>0$: otherwise $P$ would be the point mass at zero. Also $q<1$, because $\tanh^2 y<1$ for every finite $y$. We use the following dimensionless parameters: <a id="eq:entropy-scalars"></a>$$k=\frac{jq}{s}\in(0,1],\qquad C=\frac{ja}{s},\qquad
 B=jb\in(0,1),\qquad a_0=1-B\in(0,1).
 \label{eq:entropy-scalars}$$ In these parameters, multiplying the desired inequality by $j$ gives the target $$jD\ge \frac{(C-B)^2}{2(1+k)}.
 \label{eq:entropy-target}$$

We will combine two entropy bounds. A monotone change of variables handles one range of the discrepancy $C-B$; comparison with a Gaussian of a different variance handles the remaining range. We retain strictness in both arguments to identify all equality cases.

<a id="pm-h-19"></a>
###### A change-of-variables bound.

<a id="eq:entropy-slack"></a>
For the change of variables, fix $u<1$. The map $$T_u(y)=y-u\tanh y$$ is an increasing $C^1$ diffeomorphism of $\mathbb R$ onto itself, with derivative $T_u'(y)=1-u(1-m(y)^2)>0$. Change of variables in relative entropy yields $$\begin{aligned}
 D
 &=D((T_u)_\#P\Vert N(d,s))
   +\frac{2ua-u^2q}{2s}
   +\mathbb E_P\log\bigl(1-u(1-m^2)\bigr)\\
 &\ge \frac{2ua-u^2q}{2s}
   +\mathbb E_P\log\bigl(1-u(1-m^2)\bigr).
\end{aligned}$$ The entropy identity is well defined: for fixed $u<1$, $\log T_u'$ is bounded, and the second-moment assumption makes the difference of the Gaussian log densities integrable. To estimate the logarithmic term, apply concavity on the segment joining $1$ and $1-u$. For $0\le v\le1$, this gives $$\log(1-uv)\ge v\log(1-u).$$ Consequently, with $\ell(u)=-\log(1-u)-u$, <a id="eq:entropy-transport"></a>$$jD\ge u(C-B)-\frac{k u^2}{2}-B\ell(u),\qquad u<1.
 \label{eq:entropy-transport}$$ Completing the square isolates the amount by which this bound can exceed the target in <a href="#eq:entropy-target" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:entropy-target">[eq:entropy-target]</a>: $$jD-\frac{(C-B)^2}{2(1+k)}
 \ge h(u)-\frac{\bigl(C-B-(1+k)u\bigr)^2}{2(1+k)},
 \qquad h(u)=\frac{u^2}{2}-B\ell(u).
 \label{eq:entropy-slack}$$

<a id="eq:entropy-slack-positive"></a>
We first locate a range in which the extra term $h(u)$ is strictly positive: $$h(u)>0\quad\text{for }u\le a_0,\ u\ne0,
 \qquad h(a_0)\ge\frac{a_0^3}{6}.
 \label{eq:entropy-slack-positive}$$ For the negative range, integrate $v/(1+v)\le v$ over $0\le v\le -u$ to obtain $\ell(u)\le u^2/2$. Since $B<1$, this proves strict positivity for $u<0$. For the positive range, use $\ell(u)=\sum_{r\ge2}u^r/r$: it shows that $\ell(u)/u^2$ is increasing on $0<u<1$. Moreover, using $B=1-a_0$, $$h(a_0)=\frac{a_0^2}{2}-(1-a_0)\ell(a_0)
       =\sum_{r\ge3}\frac{a_0^r}{r(r-1)}
       \ge\frac{a_0^3}{6}.$$ It follows that $h(u)/u^2\ge h(a_0)/a_0^2>0$ for $0<u\le a_0$, proving <a href="#eq:entropy-slack-positive" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:entropy-slack-positive">[eq:entropy-slack-positive]</a>.

We now choose the transport parameter.
